It basically started right when the mortgage became real.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to that 'lit up' stretch earlier this month, can you tell me specifically how many days in a row you felt that way before your mood shifted back down?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say the exact number of days. It's hard to keep track when things are shifting around like that. I just know it was a few days in a row before I crashed again.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about that 'lit up' stretch, can you recall if it lasted through more than one weekend, or maybe from one workweek into the next, to help us figure out roughly how many days it went on?\n\nPatient: I really don't remember if it crossed over a weekend or anything like that. Like I said, it was just a few days in a row where I felt that way, and then it stopped. I'm sorry, I just don't have a timeline that specific.\n\nDoctor: During that 'lit up' stretch, did the energy and irritability cause any serious problems—like trouble at work, conflicts with people, or needing to be hospitalized—or was it more something only you noticed?\n\nPatient: I wasn't hospitalized or anything extreme like that, and I am still getting to work. Like I said, it was just a few days in a row where I felt that way, and then it stopped. Like, I'd just snap at my mom or even my supervisor at work over these tiny little questions that normally wouldn't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: During that time earlier this month when you felt unstoppable and irritable, how many days in a row did that last?\n\nPatient: I'm not completely sure on the exact number of days in a row it was, but... the really obvious cycling between that charged-up stretch and this crash has been going on for about a month now. It basically started right when the mortgage became real.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to that 'lit up' stretch earlier this month, can you tell me specifically how many days in a row you felt that way before your mood shifted back down?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say the exact number of days. It's hard to keep track when things are shifting around like that. I just know it was a few days in a row before I crashed again.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about that 'lit up' stretch, can you recall if it lasted through more than one weekend, or maybe from one workweek into the next, to help us figure out roughly how many days it went on?\n\nPatient: I really don't remember if it crossed over a weekend or anything like that. Like I said, it was just a few days in a row where I felt that way, and then it stopped. I'm sorry, I just don't have a timeline that specific.\n\nDoctor: During that 'lit up' stretch, did the energy and irritability cause any serious problems—like trouble at work, conflicts with people, or needing to be hospitalized—or was it more something only you noticed?\n\nPatient: I wasn't hospitalized or anything extreme like that, and I am still getting to work. But... it is causing some issues, yeah. I've made enough reconciliation mistakes lately that my supervisor actually pulled me aside to ask what was going on. Um... lately, I just wake up with this really heavy, empty feeling. It's like... even just sitting around in my own condo, which I worked so hard for, it just makes me want to cry.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, empty feeling and the sleep trouble—would you say it's been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's been a lot longer than two weeks. Um... I'd say this depressed, guilty kind of fog has actually been hanging around for about five months now. It just... it got much worse after I bought the condo.\n\nDoctor: Over these past five months, have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Um... I actually stopped going to my salsa class completely. And my paints are still sitting out on the table, but... I just look at them and feel nothing. It's honestly like they belong to somebody else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period, lasting several days or more, where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than usual, or felt on top of the world or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. Um, earlier this month, there were a few days where I felt... I don't know, almost lit up, like I was just unstoppable. I had all this energy. But then, I'd also get really irritable. Like, I'd just snap at my mom or even my supervisor at work over these tiny little questions that normally wouldn't bother me at all.\n\nDoctor: During that time earlier this month when you felt unstoppable and irritable, how many days in a row did that last?\n\nPatient: I'm not completely sure on the exact number of days in a row it was, but... the really obvious cycling between that charged-up stretch and this crash has been going on for about a month now. It basically started right when the mortgage became real.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to that 'lit up' stretch earlier this month, can you tell me specifically how many days in a row you felt that way before your mood shifted back down?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say the exact number of days. It's hard to keep track when things are shifting around like that. I just know it was a few days in a row before I crashed again.\n\nDoctor: Thinking about that 'lit up' stretch, can you recall if it lasted through more than one weekend, or maybe from one workweek into the next, to help us figure out roughly how many days it went on?\n\nPatient: I really don't remember if it crossed over a weekend or anything like that. Like I said, it was just a few days in a row where I felt that way, and then it stopped. I'm sorry, I just don't have a timeline that specific.\n\nDoctor: During that 'lit up' stretch, did the energy and irritability cause any serious problems—like trouble at work, conflicts with people, or needing to be hospitalized—or was it more something only you noticed?\n\nPatient: I wasn't hospitalized or anything extreme like that, and I am still getting to work. But... it is causing some issues, yeah. I've made enough reconciliation mistakes lately that my supervisor actually pulled me aside to ask what was going on. And outside of work, I've just been avoiding my friends and my regular salsa class. I just... I don't really feel like myself, so I've been keeping my distance from people.\n\nDoctor: During those few days when you felt 'lit up' and irritable, did you notice you needed a lot less sleep than usual but still felt full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. There were a few nights during that time where I only got maybe three or four hours of sleep. But when I woke up, I didn't feel tired at all. I felt completely charged, like I didn't even need my morning coffee.\n\nDoctor: During that 'lit up' stretch, did you notice yourself feeling unusually confident or having grand ideas about yourself—like feeling more important, talented, or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I just... I honestly started thinking I could do it all. I got it in my head that I could pay my condo down really fast, and, um, study for and pass the CPA exams. I even thought I could run a little bookkeeping business on the side while doing all that. It just felt like failure wasn't an option, like I was suddenly capable of handling anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you try to pinpoint the day this 'lit up' feeling started and the day it ended by thinking about what else was happening then—like a specific weekday, a payday, or an appointment—so we can figure out if it lasted about four days or more?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not sure about the exact days for that one specific stretch... but the whole pattern, you know, bouncing between feeling completely charged up and then crashing like this, it's been pretty obvious for about a month now. It really started right around the time my mortgage actually went through and became real. That's when I noticed it all kicking off.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned planning to sign up for CPA classes and pay down your condo faster during that lit-up time—do you recall over how many separate days you were actively pursuing those plans before your mood shifted back down?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, I didn't feel like I was acting like a totally different person. It really just felt like... I guess just a string of really good days. I was just getting my chores done and feeling a lot more upbeat than usual. I don't think it was anything where someone would look at me and say I wasn't being myself.
